->OR,<-
## An OPEN ENTRANCE

<summary>This is the title page for "Secrets Revealed," a highly influential alchemical treatise attributed to Eirenaeus Philalethes, claiming to disclose the path to the Philosophers' Stone. It establishes the author's persona as an anonymous but enlightened world citizen who achieved the Great Work at a remarkably young age.</summary>

# The Epistle Dedicatory.

*...son of honor; but having had sufficient experience of my few friends during my long troubles <note>The author likely refers to the political and personal upheavals of the English Civil War and its aftermath</note>, I instead presumed here upon the glory of your honor and virtues, rather than any personal acquaintance of my own.* Now, my **Lord**, I must ask for your pardon that such an obscure person as myself should offer so worthy a **present** <note>original: "Present," referring to the book or manuscript being dedicated</note> to your **honor**. Because of this, I have used the best lawful **strategy** <note>original: "Policy," here meaning a calculated tactic or method</note>, though perhaps a bit impolite <note>original: "unmannerly"</note>, to conceal my identity, remaining only,

1668.

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page begins the Publisher's Epistle, following the Dedicatory Epistle by W.C. While the previous section was addressed to Lord Lucas, this section addresses the general English reader, introducing the history of the manuscript and the identity of its mysterious author.</meta>

# THE
# Publisher's EPISTLE
## TO THE
# ENGLISH READER.

*Reader,*

I have enjoyed the benefit of this most excellent and exceptionally learned anonymous work <note>original: *Anonymon*, a Greek term for a nameless or anonymous writing</note> in an English manuscript for many years. This was long before its publication in Latin by the worthy and learned John Langius <note>Johann Lange (1629–1715), a German physician and scholar who published many alchemical works</note>. My version was likely transcribed from the author’s own original copy, or is at least very little corrupted. In this work, the author was pleased to reveal that he was born an Englishman, naming himself *Eirenaeus Philalethes Cosmopolita* <note>original: *Eyreneus Philaletha Cosmopolita*; a famous alchemical pseudonym meaning "a peaceful lover of truth, a citizen of the world"</note>. He also stated that he accomplished this miraculous treasure <note>referring to the alchemical secrets of the "Philosopher's Stone" described in the text</note> in 1645, when he was only twenty-three years old. 

Considering also, along with the worthy Langius, the author’s good intention to promote the welfare of all people, I could not neglect my duty...

<summary>The publisher introduces an English manuscript of a significant alchemical work, identifying its author as the young Englishman Eirenaeus Philalethes and noting its history before its Latin publication.</summary>
<keywords>Eirenaeus Philalethes, Johann Lange, alchemy, manuscript, 1645, authorship, Publisher's Epistle, English Alchemist</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the Publisher's Epistle, following the previous page where the publisher explained his decision to release this English version of the manuscript.</meta>

...longer to make his own countrymen happy through it, many of whose abilities do not extend to the *Latin language* <note>original: *Latin Tongue*</note>, whose needs and good intentions may nevertheless be suited to receive these benefits. Given the misery of these **times** requiring such aid, I hope it pleases God to give his blessing in this, our *English* shell of **learning** <note>The publisher uses "shell" metaphorically to describe the English translation that contains the "meat" or core of the alchemical knowledge</note>. I must testify along with *Langius* <note>Johann Lange (1629–1715), the German physician and scholar who published the Latin edition of this work</note> that I have hardly ever read any **author** more complete and clear regarding all operations of this **art** <note>referring to the "Hermetic Art" or alchemy</note>. 

And (which is even more to be admired and honored) he appears to be so honest and free from **envy** <note>In alchemical literature, "envy" referred to the tradition of keeping secrets hidden from the unworthy; to be free from it meant the author was unusually open and clear</note> at such a young **age**, being (as I may say) merely a **child**, but a true **child** of **art** as well as of **nature**. Indeed, I am confident he is a true **child** of **God**, who also

<summary>The publisher explains that he is publishing the work in English to benefit his countrymen who do not know Latin, praising the author's clarity and lack of secrecy despite his young age.</summary>
<keywords>Latin language, Johann Lange, alchemical art, child of art, envy of philosophers, English translation, publisher's preface</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Publisher's Epistle, further praising the author's maturity and virtue, and introducing the translated letter by Johann Lange that follows this preface.</meta>

-> *The Publisher’s Epistle.* <-

after *Christ’s* example (our great master and doctor) may be placed among the gravest, most learned doctors. *Artephius* <note>Artephius (c. 1150) was a legendary alchemical writer who claimed to have discovered the elixir of life and lived for over a thousand years</note> thought it a great proof of his extreme old age that, after a thousand years, he became free from envy <note>original: "envy"; in alchemical tradition, this refers to "the envy of the philosophers," or the habit of concealing secrets from others</note>; but this author has accomplished the same, by grace, in a few years. This demonstrates that he possesses "gray hairs" <note>A metaphor for the wisdom and maturity usually reserved for the elderly</note> in his wisdom. 

I shall say little more of him, and indeed little needs to be said beyond what you may find in effect by reading this divine author, or what has been discussed and said already by that worthy *Langius* <note>Johann Lange (1629–1715), the physician who originally edited the Latin edition</note>, whose learned epistle <note>original: "Epistle"; a formal letter or preface</note> and dedication I have translated and joined to this work so that it might be more complete. Within this book, you may—with more ease than *Jason* <note>In Greek mythology, Jason was the hero who led the Argonauts on a perilous quest to capture the Golden Fleece</note>—

# The Publisher’s Epistle

purchase the *Golden Fleece* <note>In alchemical literature, the "Golden Fleece" symbolizes the ultimate goal of the Great Work or the discovery of the Philosopher's Stone</note>, and find *An Open Entrance* to the *Closed Palace* <note>original: "Shut-Palace"; a reference to *Introitus apertus ad occlusum regis palatium*, a famous alchemical treatise by Eirenaeus Philalethes</note> of the *King*, with an *Alchemical Salt* <note>original: "Alchahestical Salt"; the Alchahest was a hypothesized universal solvent sought by alchemists</note> or *Key*, useful in *Chemistry*: But let me advise you of one thing; when you come into possession of these secrets, do not glut yourself with the pleasures of this *World* and forget *God* or your *Neighbor*. Do not, like *Midas* <note>In Greek mythology, King Midas was cursed with the "golden touch"; in some versions of the myth, he was also given the ears of a donkey as a mark of his foolishness</note>, turn your covetous eyes into longer or larger ears; otherwise, by enjoying—or rather misusing—these present things, you may lose your future happiness in the *Kingdom of Heaven*. # The Dedicatory Epistle of John Langius, Publisher of this Author in Latin.

---

# To the most Prudent and most Excellent Man, the Lord **Gabriel Vogtius**, Heir of *Elbersdorf, etc.* Privy Counselor of the most Serene and Powerful Elector of *Saxony*, and Warden of his Mint and Mining Affairs; as also Secretary of his Treasury:

->*John Langius* wishes much happiness.<- <note>original: *salutem plurimam dicit*; a standard formal greeting in Latin dedications</note>

My Lord and unique Patron;

**S**ince your Honor has been pleased to allow the <term>Candidates of Nature</term> <note>Those seeking to uncover the hidden secrets of natural philosophy and alchemy</note> to find some favor with...

<summary>In this dedication, John Langius addresses Gabriel Vogtius, a prominent Saxon official overseeing mining and finance, seeking his patronage for the publication of this alchemical work.</summary>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the Dedicatory Epistle from John Langius to Gabriel Vogtius. It explains Langius's reasons for dedicating a work written by someone else to his patron, using the metaphor of offering exotic harvests to the gods.</meta>

# The Dedicatory Epistle

...favor with you, and have lately welcomed me (though I am unworthy) with exceptional kindness; I come to offer this precise little *Work* on the true *Art* of *Chemistry* <note>original: "Chymistry"</note> to your *Honor*. I truly confess that it is the work of *another man*, but please allow me to use it to compensate for my own limitations. I am conscious of the fact that I could not provide any worthy material from my own resources that would be suitable for your accomplished *Mind*. 

Those who lack resources at home have a need to borrow goods from elsewhere. Yet, I hope you will not look down on this gift, seeing as many people have offered things to the gods that were not their own, but were taken from the *Sabean Harvest* <note>The "Sabean Harvest" refers to the precious frankincense and spices from the ancient kingdom of Sheba (Saba), often used in religious rituals. The author is suggesting that even a "borrowed" gift can be high in value.</note>. This is especially true since this *Excellent Book* seems to have been produced from the favorable bosom of *Nature* with a clear and good intention. Therefore, it could not be—nor indeed ought it to be—anything but

->most<-

<summary>John Langius humbles himself before his patron, Gabriel Vogtius, explaining that although he did not write this book on chemistry himself, he offers it as a valuable "harvest" of natural knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>John Langius, Gabriel Vogtius, chemistry, Sabean harvest, patronage, natural philosophy, dedication</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page concludes the Dedicatory Epistle from John Langius to Gabriel Vogtius, transitioning from the description of the book's value to a formal closing and date.</meta>

->most<-

acceptable to you, whom *Nature* long ago counted among her intimate friends. Your house has until now been (like *Parnassus* and *Helicon* <note>Parnassus and Helicon are mountains in Greek mythology sacred to the Muses, representing centers of art and learning.</note>) open to all the lovers and friends of the *Muses* and the *Graces* <note>The Muses were the goddesses of inspiration in the arts and sciences; the Graces represented charm, beauty, and creativity.</note>. Therefore, let it be open to this work of ours, which is not at all ragged—as if it crept out of the *Trophonian Den* <note>original: "Trophonian Den". This refers to the cave of the oracle Trophonius, where visitors were said to emerge looking pale and terrified. Langius is saying this book is clear and bright, not obscure or frightening.</note> to beg for *charity* <note>original: "Alms"</note>—but instead truly has a respectable appearance <note>original: "Habit"</note>, an open and clear face, and a radiant gown. It comes to you from the most private study <note>original: "retired'st Closet"</note> of *Chemistry* <note>original: "Chymistry"</note> to call upon your *Honor*. Therefore, receive it kindly; for if you welcome it properly, I do not doubt that (like a grateful *guest*) it will return rare *gifts* to you. I pray *God* that you may live many, many years for your own comfort and for the hopes of your family, of *Nature*, and of our public.

*Hamburg*, December 9, in the year (according to the common calendar <note>original: "vulgar accompt"</note>) 1666.

<summary>John Langius concludes his dedication by praising Gabriel Vogtius’s home as a center of learning and presenting the book as a bright, sophisticated work of chemistry rather than an obscure or "ragged" text.</summary>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>This page concludes a dedicatory epistle by John Lancius (also known as Jan Lancy or John Langius), transitioning from the praise of the recipient to the formal closing of the letter.</meta>

# Of JOHN LANCIUS.

...most acceptable to you, whom *Nature* long ago gave a title to through her intimate friends. Your house has until now been (like *Parnassus* and *Helicon* <note>Parnassus and Helicon are mountains in Greek mythology sacred to the Muses, representing centers of art, music, and learning.</note>) open to all the lovers and friends of the *Muses* and the *Graces* <note>The Muses were the nine goddesses of inspiration in the arts and sciences; the Graces were goddesses of charm, beauty, and creativity.</note>. Therefore, let it be open to this work of ours, which is not at all ragged—as if it crept out of *Trophonius's Den* <note>original: "Trophonius Den." This refers to the cave of the oracle Trophonius, where those who entered were said to return appearing pale and terrified. Lancius is suggesting this book is clear and inviting, not dark or confusing.</note> to beg for *charity* <note>original: "Alms"</note>—but instead truly has a respectable appearance <note>original: "decent Habit"</note>, an open and clean face, and a superior gown. It comes to you from the well-known study <note>original: "noted Closet"</note> of *Chemistry* <note>original: "Chymistry"</note> to call upon your Honor. Therefore, receive it kindly; for if you welcome it correctly, I do not doubt that (like a grateful guest) it will return the favor to you. I pray God you may live many, many years for your own comfort and for the hopes of your family, the *Muses*, and our public.

->Danzig <note>original: "Dantzick"</note>, December 3, in the year (according<-
->to the common calendar <note>original: "vulgar account"</note>) 1664.<-

<summary>John Lancius concludes his dedication by praising the recipient's home as a sanctuary for learning and presenting his work as a sophisticated, clear contribution to the field of chemistry.</summary>
<keywords>John Lancius, Parnassus, Helicon, Muses, Graces, Trophonius, Chemistry, Danzig, 1664</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the preface to the reader by John Langius (also known as John Lancius), following the dedicatory epistle from the previous page. Langius introduces the book and explains his motivation for publishing it.</meta>

### T H E
## Preface of John Langius
### T O T H E
# READER.

*Benevolent Reader,*

Having recently <note>original: "not long since"</note> obtained this small book—which I am publishing here—from a man highly knowledgeable in these subjects, I found it so pleasing that I immediately decided to share it for your benefit. I was motivated by the fact that this true author seemed to be almost the only person in our age to possess and produce the Physical Tincture <term>The Physical Tincture, or the Philosopher's Stone, was a legendary alchemical substance said to be capable of turning base metals into gold or serving as a universal medicine.</term>. He seems to have written his work for the specific purpose of being recognized as an Adept <term>An Adept (or Adeptist) was a master who had achieved the highest secrets of alchemy.</term> when the book was released, and so that those wandering lost in this art might be brought back <note>original: "reduced"</note> to

<summary>John Langius introduces his publication of an alchemical treatise, praising the author as a rare master (Adept) of the Physical Tincture who intended to guide others lost in the study of alchemy.</summary>
<keywords>John Langius, Preface, Alchemy, Physical Tincture, Adeptist, Philosopher's Stone, Alchemical Education</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by John Langius, discussing the author's style in relation to Michael Sendivogius and the benefit the book will provide to those seeking the Philosopher's Elixir.</meta>

-> *The Preface of John Langius.* <-

the right path. Added to this is the clarity and transparency <note>original: "candour and perspicuity"</note> of the *writing*, which seems to follow the *style* of *Sendivogius* <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636) was a famous Polish alchemist and philosopher known for his work "A New Light of Alchemy."</note>, whose footsteps this *author* follows closely everywhere—indeed, he sometimes excels them—and always explains him excellently without even seeming to try. 

Therefore, I was confident that if I were to publish <note>original: "divulge"</note> it, both the author's aforementioned noble goal would be fulfilled, and a clear light would be kindled in the dark night of *errors* for many people who, to this very day, hunt for the great *Elixir of the Philosophers* <term>The Elixir of the Philosophers, similar to the Philosopher's Stone, was a legendary substance believed to grant longevity and transform base metals into gold.</term> through painful *labor*, arrogant *learning*, or constant, unending *sighs* (though, alas, their hopes fail them from time to time). For although

<summary>John Langius praises the author’s clear writing style, comparing it to the famous alchemist Michael Sendivogius, and expresses his belief that publishing this work will guide those struggling to discover the Philosopher's Elixir.</summary>
<keywords>John Langius, Michael Sendivogius, Alchemy, Elixir of the Philosophers, Alchemical style, Philosopher's Stone</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by John Langius, following his praise for the author's clarity and his hope that the book will guide those searching for the Philosopher's Elixir.</meta>

-> *The Preface of John Langius.* <-

Within these past few years, a number of people have ambitiously tried to claim the title of "True Philosophy." Under the mask <note>original: "vizard"</note> of a false name, they have presumed to instruct—or rather, to distract and drive the whole world mad—with fanciful <note>original: "phantastick"</note> books. They have even drawn some ignorant men (and some quite well-known ones, at that) onto the public stage, much to the laughter of those who are more sensible and learned. 

Nevertheless, those with sharper wits—and those who have "conversed" more closely with "dead philosophers" <note>This refers to studying the classic texts of ancient or deceased scholars.</note> (as the proverb goes)—soon discovered their tricks and traps <note>original: "hooks and baits"</note>. Consequently, shortly afterward, those who made such grand promises and spoke such puffed-up <note>original: "swel’d"</note> words were abandoned by their admirers. They were like the donkey who was stripped of the lion’s skin <note>A reference to one of Aesop's Fables, where an ass dresses as a lion to scare others but is eventually exposed.</note>. And being 

<summary>John Langius criticizes the rise of fraudulent alchemical and philosophical books, noting that while they may fool the ignorant, experienced scholars quickly recognize them as deceptive "traps."</summary>
<keywords>True Philosophy, alchemy, fraudulent books, dead philosophers, Aesop's fables, lion's skin, John Langius</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues Langius's critique of fraudulent authors, comparing this new, anonymous work to a rising sun that will dispel the "fog" of false alchemy.</meta>

-> *The Preface of John Langius.* <-

Left to themselves, they have always paid their share <note>original: "born their shot"</note> among false counterfeiters <note>original: "Coiners"</note> and paid every last penny <note>original: "the utmost farthing"</note>.

Truly, as to who or what kind of person was the author of this sweet, fresh <note>original: "Must-like," referring to *must*, or newly pressed wine before fermentation</note> work, I know no more than the most ignorant person. # The Author's Preface

Being an anonymous adept <note>original: "Adept Anonymon," referring to a master of alchemy who remains unnamed</note>, a lover of learning, and a philosopher, I decided to write this little treatise of medicinal, chemical, and physical secrets <note>original: "Arcana's," referring to hidden or esoteric knowledge</note> in the year of the redemption of the world, 1645. At that time, I was in the twenty-third year of my life. I wrote this so that I might fulfill my duty to the "Sons of Art" <note>A traditional term for students and practitioners of alchemy</note> and lend my hand to lead them out of the labyrinth of errors in which they are entangled. I also wished to show other adepts that I am their brother and equal, and to help those who have been seduced by the deceits of sophists <note>original: "Sophisters"; here meaning false teachers or deceptive alchemists who promise gold but deliver nothing</note> safely return to see and embrace the true light. I also foresee that many will be enlightened by these my labors; they are no fables, but real experiments,

<summary>The anonymous author, an adept alchemist writing in 1645 at age 23, explains that he wrote this treatise to guide sincere students away from the errors of false teachers and toward true alchemical knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Adept Anonymon, Sons of Art, alchemy, chemical secrets, medicinal arcana, sophistry, 1645, enlightenment</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's explanation of his motivation for writing, shifting from his personal background to the divine compulsion he felt to reveal alchemical truths clearly for the benefit of others.</meta>

# The Author's Preface

...which I have seen, performed, and know to be true; any master of this art <note>original: "Adeptist"</note> will easily understand this from what I have written.

Therefore, as I write these things for the benefit of my neighbor, let it be enough for me to declare that no one has ever written about this art <note>The "Art" refers to alchemy and the pursuit of the Philosopher's Stone</note> so clearly. Many times while writing, I laid down my pen because I was more inclined to hide the truth under a "mask of envy" <note>original: "Mask of Envy"; a common alchemical tradition where authors intentionally obscured their recipes to keep them from the unworthy or the greedy</note>. However, God—whom I could not resist and who alone knows the heart—compelled me to write. To Him alone be glory forever.

From this, I am certain that many will be blessed in this final age of the world <note>Many 17th-century writers believed they were living in the "last age" or the end times, a period they expected would be marked by the revelation of hidden knowledge</note> with this secret knowledge <note>original: "Arcanum," meaning a deep mystery or secret of nature</note>, because I have written faithfully. Nor have I intentionally left anything confusing for a young beginner that has not been fully explained. I also know many people who, along with me, enjoy...

<summary>The author asserts that his writing is the clearest alchemical guide ever produced, revealing that he felt divinely compelled to share these secrets rather than obscuring them with traditional riddles. He believes this faithfully written "Arcanum" will enlighten many during what he considers the final age of the world.</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, Arcanum, Mask of Envy, divine inspiration, last age of the world, secret knowledge, clarity, Adeptist</keywords>

# The Author's Preface

...this secret <note>original: "Arcanum," referring to a deep mystery or hidden knowledge of nature</note>, and I am persuaded there are many more with whose new familiarity I shall shortly (as I may say) become daily acquainted. Let the holy will of God do what it pleases; I confess myself unworthy for such things to be accomplished through me. Yet in these matters, I adore the holy will of God, to whom all created things must be subject. For this purpose alone He created them, and having created them, He preserves them.

The true manuscript copy, which *John Langius* <note>Johann Lange (1485–1565) was a famous German physician and scholar whose writings often touched on alchemical and medical secrets</note> so greatly desired in his preface, is published here for your benefit. In this edition, you will find significant expansions and explanations that are lacking in the *Latin translation*, as evidenced in *Chapter 15* and elsewhere.

<margin>What our Gold is. —</margin>
—degree of purity and subtle stability <note>original: "fixity," the state of being non-volatile or resistant to change by fire</note>, to which it may be brought by Nature and a skilled artist. Gold thus transformed into its essence **is called Our Gold** (and no longer common gold) and is the ultimate point of the perfection of Nature and Art. I could cite all the philosophers who write of this thing, but I need no witnesses; because I myself, being an adept, write more clearly than anyone before me.

<margin>But our Gold [☉] which we require for our work is in nature and volatile, and is clearly Jupiter [♃] <note>original: "♃", the symbol for Jupiter/tin.</note></margin>
Let anyone believe me who will, and disprove it who can, and let any complain who wish; the reward they shall certainly receive is to be shown as profoundly ignorant. I admit that clever minds imagine many fantasies, but he who is diligent shall find the truth in the simple way of Nature.

## Page 79, Line 12.

<margin>The word "white" should be omitted. It should read: "Therefore it is joined to our more crude gold," and it is clear that this refers to our Mercury [☿] starting from line 13 of this page.</margin>

Or white and more crude gold may mean our Mercury [☿] <note>original: "☿", the symbol for mercury</note> alone; according to the last line [gloss: e] of page 22 in the book titled *Secrets Revealed*. It is a volatile gold [☉] <note>original: "☉", the symbol for gold</note> that brings forth its seed <note>original: "Sperme"</note>, and eventually both unite with an unbreakable <note>original: "insoluble"</note> bond. Thus it becomes our Hermaphrodite <note>In alchemy, a "Hermaphrodite" is a substance that has reached a state of balance between active (male) and passive (female) principles.</note>, being powerful in both sexes. 

Therefore, physical gold <note>original: "corporal Gold"</note> is "dead" before it is joined with its bride. With her, the coagulating Antimony <note>original: "♁", the symbol for antimony, often used in the purification of gold</note>, which is external in gold [☉], is turned inward. In this way, the "height" <note>original: "altitude"; likely referring to the visible, physical properties</note> is hidden and the "depth" <note>original: "profundity"; the inner essence</note> is revealed. Thus, the stable substance <note>original: "the Fix"</note> is, in time, made volatile <note>volatile: able to be vaporized or changed</note>, so that it may afterward possess—by right of inheritance—a most noble state, in which it may achieve an excellent and powerful stability <note>original: "fixity"</note>. It is clear, therefore, that the entire secret lies in Mercury [☿], of which a philosopher says:

> In Mercury is found everything the wise man seeks;

Concerning which Geber <note>Geber: The Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan, a legendary 8th-century figure considered the father of chemistry and alchemy.</note> says:

<margin>Observe: "overcoming all things" means overcoming all metals.</margin>

> Praised be the Most High, who has created our Mercury [☿] and has given it a nature that overcomes all things.

For truly, if that were not so, the alchemists might boast all they like, but their work of alchemy would be useless. It is also evident that this is not common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> mercury, but the Sophic <note>Sophic: from "Sophia" (wisdom); a refined, philosophical substance used by alchemists rather than the raw metal found in shops.</note> Mercury. Every common mercury is "male"—that is, physical, specific, and "dead." But our Mercury is spiritual, feminine, living, and life-giving. Pay attention, therefore, to what I shall say regarding Mercury [☿]; for as the philoso— <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely continuing as "philosopher."</note>

## CHAPTER 7.
### *Concerning the First Operation of Preparing the Philosophical Mercury by the Flying Eagles.*

Brother, you should know that our exact knowledge of the "Eagles of the Philosophers" <note>In alchemy, "Eagles" usually refer to the cycles of distillation or sublimation used to purify a substance. Each "flight" of an eagle represents one cycle of purification</note> is considered the first degree of perfection; for to understand it, quick ingenuity is required. Do not believe that this science comes to any of us by chance or through a random imagination, as the common ignorant people foolishly believe. Instead, we have labored long and hard; we have spent many sleepless nights and have undergone much toil and sweat to obtain the truth.

Therefore, diligent beginner, know for certain that you will accomplish nothing without labor and sweat in the "First Work" <term>The initial, laborious stage of the alchemical process involving the physical preparation and purification of primary materials</term>. However, in the "Second Work" <term>The stage of the process that occurs after the materials are sealed in the vessel, where nature is said to complete the transformation on its own</term>, Nature alone performs the work without any manual labor <note>original: "imposition of hands"</note>, using only a moderate external fire.

Understand, therefore, Brother, the sayings of the *Sophi* <note>original: "Sophi"; from the Greek *sophoi*, meaning the "wise men" or masters of alchemy</note> when they write that their Eagles are to be brought—

<meta>The text ends here on a catchword "to," which will begin the next page's description of the process of bringing the "Eagles" to the matter.</meta>

<summary>This page introduces Chapter 7, emphasizing that the "First Work" of alchemy requires intense personal labor and intellectual effort, specifically regarding the purification process known as the "Eagles." The author distinguishes this difficult manual stage from the "Second Work," where nature completes the process on its own.</summary>

<keywords>Sophic Mercury, Flying Eagles, First Work, Second Work, Sophi, distillation, sublimation, alchemical labor</keywords>

<margin>Some use more "Eagles," some fewer, according to the quantity of matter used each time.</margin>
<margin>A person may put in exactly enough matter all at once, but that is not strictly necessary and requires great practice.</margin>

...to devour the Lion <note>In alchemy, the "Lion" often represents fixed matter, such as gold or antimony, which must be "devoured" or dissolved by the solvent</note>. The fewer "Eagles" there are, the harder the struggle and the slower the victory; however, the work is most excellently perfected in the seventh or ninth cycle. 

The Sophic Mercury <note>original: ☿ Sophical</note>, specifically, is the "Bird of Hermes," which is sometimes called a "Goose," and sometimes a "Pheasant"—it is called one thing now, and another thing then. But whenever the Magi <term>Magi: the master alchemists or "wise men" of the tradition</term> speak of their "Eagles," they always speak in the plural, and they assign their number as being from three to ten. Yet, they are not to be understood as if they required that many specific weights or parts of the water to one part of the earth. Instead, you must interpret their sayings as referring to the internal <margin>By "Eagle" is meant the internal quality of the Mercury.</margin> quality <gloss>quality of the Mercury <note>original: ☿</note></gloss> of the weight. That is to say, you must take the water and "sharpen" or strengthen it as many times as they number their Eagles. 

This sharpening is achieved by repeated refining <gloss>repetition upon our matter</gloss> through heat <term>sublimation: the process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then cools back into a solid, used here to describe the cycles of purification</term>. Therefore, let every refining <gloss>proposition</gloss> of the Philosophical Mercury <note>original: ☿</note> be considered one "Eagle." The seventh common <gloss>vulgar</gloss> refining will exalt the Mercury so much that it will become a most suitable "Bath" <term>Bath: a chemical solvent or environment in which the "King" (gold) can be dissolved and transformed</term> for your **King**. 

<margin>Note Well</margin> Therefore, so that you may have this mystery <note>original: "knot"</note> clearly explained, pay close attention. Take our "Fiery Dragon" <term>Fiery Dragon: a symbolic name for a primary raw material, often associated with crude ores or mineral acids</term> <margin>our first matter</margin>, which hides the "Magical Steel" <term>Magical Chalybs: a term for the "soul" or essential essence of iron or steel used in the alchemical process</term> in his <margin>soul of Mars <note>original: ♂</note></margin>...

<margin>By the "Eagles" the author means each soaking <term>imbibition: the process of a solid absorbing a liquid</term>. For when the matter is dried out by decomposition <term>Putrefaction: a stage in the alchemical process where the matter breaks down, often turning black</term>, it is no longer "flying" (volatile). But then, a new soaking dissolves it again, and then it is once more an "Eagle" or "Bird."</margin>

# CHAPTER 8.
## Regarding the labor and tediousness of the first Preparation.

**S**ome ignorant chemists dream that the entire work, from the beginning to the end, is a mere recreation, full of pleasantness; they set the labor aside, outside the boundaries of this art. But let them safely enjoy their own opinion in a work which they have imagined to be so easy; certainly, they will reap only an empty harvest from their...

# Secrets Revealed.

...idle operation. For we know that, after the Divine Blessing and a good foundation to work upon, labor, industry, and diligence take the highest priority. Truly, it is not such an easy task that it might be called child’s play or a mental relaxation, if it is to give us the thing we so earnestly desire. Instead, as *Hermes* <note>original: Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many alchemical texts are attributed</note> says, *“Neither life nor labor should be spared”*; otherwise, what the *Wise Man* <note>King Solomon, traditionally credited with the Book of Proverbs</note> foretold in his parables will come true: namely, *“The desire of the lazy man will destroy him.”* Nor is it any wonder that so many men who deal with *Alchemy* <note>original: Alchymy</note> are reduced to poverty; for they avoid hard work and spare <gloss>no</gloss> expense. But we, who have known these things and performed them, have certainly found that no labor is more tedious than our first preparation. ### CHAPTER 12.

...has shown these Secrets of Nature to us, which he has hidden from the eyes of most men. Those things, therefore, which are freely given to us by that great Giver, we will reveal freely and faithfully to other studious men. Know, therefore, that the greatest secret of our Operation <term>Operation</term> <note>the specific chemical or spiritual process of alchemy</note> is nothing other than a cohobation <term>cohobation</term> <note>the process of repeatedly distilling a liquid from the same solid substance to concentrate its power</note> of the natures of one thing over the other, until the most digested virtue <term>virtue</term> <note>the potent medicinal or transformative power of a substance</note> is extracted out of the digested body by the crude <note>unrefined or raw</note> one. 

But for this, there is required: first, an exact preparation and fitness of the things that enter into the Work; second, a good arrangement of external things; third, once things are thus prepared, there is required a good **Regimen** <note>original: *Regimen*; the systematic control of heat and timing in the alchemical process</note>; fourth, a foreknowledge of the appearances <note>the visual signs, such as color changes, that indicate progress</note> in the Work is required, so that your procedure is not performed blindfolded; fifth, Patience, so that the Work is not hurried or managed recklessly. We will speak of all these things in order, as one brother to another.

Just as wheat sown in the ground changes its name and is called the farmer’s seed-corn (whether for bread, other uses, or for seed), so it is with gold. As long as it takes the form of a ring, a vessel, or money, it is common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> gold; but once it is cast into our "water," it is "philosophical" gold. In the first case, it is called "dead" because it would remain unchanged even until the end of the world. In the second case, it is said to be "living" because it has that potential. This potential can be activated <note>original: "brought into Act"</note> within a few days, but then the gold will no longer be gold, but the *Chaos* of the *Sophi* <note>original: "Chaos of the Sophi"; the "Chaos of the Wise Men," referring to the primordial, unformed state of matter necessary for the Great Work.</note>. For just as a man is said to be dead when he has already received a death sentence, so gold is said to be alive when it is mixed in such a composition and placed over such a fire that it will necessarily receive a germinating life <note>original: "germinative life"; life capable of sprouting or growing</note> in a short time. Therefore, the *Magi* <note>original: "Magi"; the masters or wise practitioners of alchemy</note> command you to revive the dead and to kill the living. At the very beginning, they call their water "living," and they say that the death of one principle occurs at the same time <note>original: "hath one and the same period"</note> as the death of the other.

Mercury <note>original: ☿</note> prepared in this way is nowhere to be found upon the earth.
</margin>
The mystery which we so carefully hide is how to prepare the Mercury <note>original: ☿</note>, truly so called, which cannot be found upon the earth ready-made for our use; and this is for unique reasons known only to the Adepts <note>original: "Adeptists"; those who have mastered the secret of the Philosopher's Stone</note>.

<margin>
The work is performed on common Gold <note>original: ☉</note>.
</margin>

<margin>
If you are ignorant of the Mercury of the Philosophers, or of purged Gold <note>original: ☉</note>, add this to extract it out of common Gold, which may be done in 140 or 150 days. This is called the first preparation; see <note>original: "vid."; Latin "vide" meaning "see"</note> pages 66, 71, 72, 73, and 74.
</margin>
In this Mercury, we carefully <gloss>boil</gloss> <note>original: "Boyle"</note> and combine <note>original: "amalgamate"</note> pure gold, refined to the highest degree of purity and either filed or beaten into thin sheets. Being sealed in the glass, we boil it daily. The gold is dissolved by the power of our "Water," and it returns to its most basic form <note>original: "nearest matter"</note>. In this state, the hidden life of the gold becomes free and takes on the life of the dissolving Mercury, which (compared to the gold) is like good soil compared to a grain of wheat. 

In this Mercury, therefore, once the gold is dissolved, it putrefies—and it must necessarily do so by the laws of nature. Therefore, after the decay of death, a new body arises. It is of the same essence as the former body, but of a more noble substance, which takes on degrees of power <note>original: "virtuality"; the inherent strength or medicinal potency of the substance</note>...

<margin>
This is the Mercury of the Philosophers' Gold, which you may obtain from the Philosophical Mercury by heating <note>original: "Digestion"</note> for a week; see page 72.
</margin>

### CHAPTER 16.

### Of the Amalgamation of the Mercury and Gold, and of the due weight of both.

<gloss>All of this chapter concerns the same subject as part of the previous one and is considered <term>spurious</term> <note>original: Spurious; suggesting that this section may have been added by a later editor or is not part of the original authentic manuscript.</note>.</gloss>

### CHAPTER 17.
## Regarding the Preparation, Form, Matter, and Closing of the Vessel.

You shall have an oval or round glass vessel, large enough to hold at most (in its sphere or belly) one ounce of distilled water. Do not use a smaller size if you can avoid it, but get it as near to that measure as possible. Let the glass have a neck with a height of one palm, or a hand-breadth, or a span <gloss>or 9 or 10 inches</gloss>. Let the glass be clear and thick—the thicker the better, provided it is clear and clean enough to observe the actions occurring within it. It must not be thicker in one place than in another. 

<margin>The work in common gold.</margin>
The proportion of matter for this glass should be half an ounce of Gold with one ounce of Mercury <note>original: ☿</note>, which is a ratio of two to one. Even if you add three parts of Mercury to one part of Gold, the entire compound will still be less <insert>than</insert> two ounces, and this proportion is excellent. Furthermore, unless the glass is strong, it will not hold up in the fire against the winds <note>The "winds" refer to the internal pressure of expanding gases or vapors during heating.</note> that are created in the vessel during the forming of our *Embryo* <note>The "Embryo" refers to the early stage of the alchemical Stone or the developing metallic compound.</note>, which will easily

### CHAPTER 18.
## Of the Philosophical Furnace or Athanor <note>Athanor: a self-feeding furnace designed to maintain a uniform heat over a long period.</note>.

We have spoken of Mercury <note>original: ☿</note>, its preparation, proportion, and virtue. We have also spoken of Sulfur <note>original: 🜍</note>, its necessity and use in our Work. I have shown how these are to be prepared and taught how they are to be mixed. I have also revealed much concerning the vessel in which they are to be sealed. All of these instructions are to be understood with a grain of salt <note>original: cum grano salis</note>; otherwise, if you proceed too literally, you may frequently happen to err, which the unusual nature of this work can often cause. For we have so mingled our philosophical subtleties with unusual frankness <note>original: candor</note> that unless you sniff out the many *metaphors* in our preceding chapters, your *harvest* will hardly result in anything better than the loss of time, money, and effort. For example: we told you without any ambiguity that one of our Principles was Mercury <note>original: ☿</note>, and the other <gloss>this our matter</gloss> was a substance commonly available for sale; the other <gloss>our Gold</gloss> <note>original: ☉</note> is to be made by our Art. Yet do not misunderstand me, for our Gold is, by all <margin>when made.</margin> tests, good Gold; therefore it is "saleable"—that is, if it were reduced to a metal, it could be sold without any hesitation. 

But our Gold is not to be bought with money, even if you would give a crown or a kingdom for it, for it is the gift of God. Our Gold is not to be found ready-made (at least not commonly). Before it becomes "our Gold" <note>original: ☉</note>, it requires our Art. Yet you may also seek our Gold <margin>see page 44.</margin> in common Gold <note>original: ☉</note> and common Silver <note>original: ☽</note> and find it, if you seek correctly. 

So then, our Gold is the "proximate matter" <term>the immediate starting material</term> for our Stone. Common Gold and Silver are "near matters," but other metals are "remote matters," and those things which are not metallic are "most remote"—that is, completely foreign to the work. # Secrets Revealed.

We extract our true Matter out of any common metal; <margin>Not put to flight by the fire.</margin> for our <gloss>own matter</gloss> Gold is a Chaos, whose soul is not put to flight by the fire; but common Gold is a body whose soul has retired into a stronghold, so that it may be defended there from the violence of the fire. Therefore, the Philosophers say that the Fire of Vulcan <note>original: "Fire of Vulcan"; refers to the intense heat of a furnace or forge</note> is the artificial death of the metals, and as many as have suffered melting <note>original: "fusion"</note> have lost their Life in it.

<margin>Apply this both to your imperfect body and to your Fiery Dragon</margin>
If you can apply <gloss>this concept</gloss> cleverly, both to your imperfect body and to your *Fiery Dragon* <note>A symbolic term in alchemy often referring to a volatile or corrosive substance, such as mercury or acidic salts, used to dissolve metals</note>, you need no other Key to all our Secrets. First, sublime <note>original: "sublime"; to heat a solid until it becomes a vapor and then solidifies again</note> and boil this compound until it is fit to be united with Virgin's Milk <note>A symbolic alchemical term usually referring to a white liquid or mercurial solvent</note>.

<margin>The secret furnace is our Mercury (☿), which contains within it a true fire.</margin>

Yet, let it be as it will, you can never do anything without fire. It was not in vain, then, that the truth-telling Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy</note>, next to the Father (the Sun/Gold ☉) and Mother (the Moon/Silver ☾), reckons the fire as the third element or the governor of the whole process. But this is also to be understood as the truly secret furnace, which a common eye never saw.

There are several sorts of furnaces; there is also another furnace which is called our common furnace. This is made either of brick or potter's clay, or of iron or copper plates well luted <note>original: "luted"; sealed with airtight clay or cement</note> within. This furnace we call an *Athanor* <note>A self-feeding furnace designed to maintain a constant heat over a long period</note>. The form that pleases me best is a tower with a "nest" <note>The part of the furnace where the flask or vessel sits</note>. Let the tower be about two feet high or more, and nine inches broad within the plates (or a common span). Let it be about two inches broad at the bottom on each side, and about seven or eight inches high at most. The area where the fire is kept should be thicker with clay than the top, but it should have a smooth, somewhat tapering ascent. Next to the bottom or foundation, let there be an ash-hole three or four fingers high, or a little more, with a grate and stone fitted to it. A little above the grate...

# Secrets Revealed.

<margin>The construction of the furnace.</margin>
Place a grate about an inch high, and let there be two holes which may provide a vent into a "nest" <note>original: "Nest"; a specialized compartment or chamber within the furnace designed to hold vessels</note>, which must be closely joined at the side. Let the holes be about an inch in diameter, and the nest should be capable of receiving three or four egg-glasses <note>original: "Egg-glasses"; oval-shaped alchemical flasks resembling eggs</note>, but not too large. Let the nest and the tower be very free from cracks, and let the nest have no open space downwards below the dish, so that the fire may come immediately under the platter, and so forth through two, three, or four holes. Let the nest have a cover with a window in it, where a glass about a foot high may stand, or else let the top of it extend out through a hole above. 

Being thus arranged, set your furnace in a well-lit place. The coals are to be put in at the top—first live ones and then others—and the top is to be shut off from all air with a cover, using sifted ashes in the joints to seal it. In such a furnace, you may perform the Work from the beginning to the end.

But if you are meticulous, you may find various other ways of administering a proper *regulation* <note>original: "Regimen"; the systematic management of heat over time</note> of fire. Let it be a general rule, then, that such an *ever-burning furnace* <note>original: "Athanor"; a self-feeding furnace designed to maintain a constant temperature for long periods</note> be made, in which, without moving the glass, you

<margin>//</margin> You may apply whatever degree of heat you desire, ranging from a "feverish" low heat to a soft reverberating heat or a dark red glow. At its highest intensity, let this heat last at least eight to ten hours without needing to be refilled with coals; any less time than that is too exhausting for the workman. Once you have achieved this, you have opened the "first gate" <note>The "first gate" refers to the successful completion of the first stage of the alchemical process.</note>.

When you have finally obtained the <term>Stone</term> <note>The Philosopher's Stone, the ultimate goal of alchemy.</note>, you may make the previously mentioned furnace portable (as I have done myself). It is easily moved, and the remaining operations are not long and tedious, but very short, and therefore do not require a large furnace. A large furnace would be difficult to carry around and more trouble than simply waking up a little earlier than usual to refill a small furnace with coals for about a week—or at most two or three weeks—during the process of <term>Multiplication</term> <note>Multiplication is the stage where the power and quantity of the Philosopher's Stone is increased.</note>.

### CHAPTER 19.
## Of the Progress of the Work in the first Forty Days.

<margin>To the sight—that is, no fumes appear.</margin>
Having prepared our Gold <note>original: "Sol"</note> and our Mercury <note>original: "☿"</note>, <gloss>Observe our Gold.</gloss> shut them in our vessels and govern them with our fire. Within forty days, you shall see your whole matter turned into a shadow or *atoms*, without any visible mover or motion, and without any heat perceptible to the touch, except only that it is hot.

<margin>This our work is Mercury.</margin>

# Secrets Revealed.

Others deny that common <term>vulgar: common or commercially available</term> gold <note>original: ☉</note> and silver <note>original: ☽</note> are used, and they also speak the truth. Being now moved by charity, I will reach forth my hand to help. In this, I dare appeal to all the *Adepts* <note>original: Adepti; those who have mastered the secret of alchemy</note> who have ever written, and I accuse them all of envy—yes, even myself, for I had resolved to tread that same path of secrecy and spite. However, God has compelled me to go beyond what I intended; to Him be everlasting praise.

I say then that each way is true, for there is but one way in the end, though not in the beginning. Our whole secret lies in our Mercury <note>original: ☿</note> and in our Gold. Our Mercury is our way, and without it, nothing is accomplished. Our Gold is also not common gold, yet our Gold is contained within common gold—otherwise, how could metals be of the same nature <note>original: homogeneal</note>? 

If, then, you know how to "illuminate" our Mercury as it ought to be, but you lack our specific Gold, you may join the Mercury with common gold.

One Work is most secret and purely natural, which is with our Mercury <note>symbol: ☿</note> and our Gold <note>symbol: Sol</note>, to which Work belong all the signs described by the philosophers. This Work is done neither by fire nor by hands, but only by internal heat; the external heat is only for expelling cold and overcoming its symptoms.

## CHAPTER 21.
### *Regarding the Burning of the Flowers, and how to prevent it.*

<margin>The word "it" refers to the vessel.</margin>
The burning of the "Flowers" <note>original: "Flowers"; a term for the volatile substances or vapors that rise and crystallize during sublimation</note> is an error of fatal consequence, yet it is easily committed. Before the "Natures" <note>the essential qualities of the substances</note>—which are tender and extracted from their depths—are stabilized, they are often burnt. This error is chiefly to be guarded against after the first three weeks. In the beginning, there is so much moisture that if the work is governed by a stronger fire than is appropriate, the glass—being brittle—will not withstand the abundance of pressurized gases but will suddenly shatter. If the glass is too large, the vapors will be so excessively dispersed that they will hardly return to their "body" <note>the solid matter at the bottom of the vessel</note> again; at least

<meta>The text continues on the next page with the word "not".</meta>

<summary>This page describes the 'Chaos' phase of the alchemical process, characterized by darkness and symbolic eclipses, and warns of the dangers of overheating the delicate 'Flowers' or vapors within the glass vessel.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Chaos, Burning of the Flowers, sublimation, heat management, chemical vessel, eclipse of the sun and moon</keywords>

--- Page 129 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the alchemical process, specifically focusing on the transition from the initial moist stage to the stabilization of the "Stone" through heat management.</meta>

# Secrets Revealed.

The water should be used only as much as is necessary for the refreshment of the Stone. But as soon as the earth shall begin to retain part of its water, and the vapors begin to decrease, the fire may be strengthened without danger of breaking the vessel. However, if the fire is too intense, the work will nevertheless be destroyed; it will take on the color of a wild poppy, and the whole compound will eventually become a dry and useless powder of a dull red color. 

You shall conclude from this sign that your fire has been too strong—so strong, in fact, as to hinder the true conjunction <note>The chemical or spiritual union of the substances.</note>. For you must know that our work requires a true change of natures, which cannot happen until an entire union of both principles is made. These principles cannot be united except in the form of water; for while solid bodies may be mixed or blended together, they cannot be truly united. Nor can any solid body be united with a spirit *at the smallest molecular level* <note>original: "per minima"</note>. 

But spirits may be easily united with other spirits. Therefore, in our operations, our <gloss>materials</gloss> must become a homogeneous metallic water. The way to achieve this solution is through our previously mentioned true calcination <note>The process of reducing a metal to a powder by heat, though here described as a specialized alchemical "moist" process.</note>. <margin>This is called Calcination</margin> This calcination is not properly a drying out <note>original: "exsiccation"</note>, but a kind of grinding into water <margin>by</margin> <gloss>using water</gloss> as earth is reduced to atoms.

### CHAPTER 22.

## *The Rule of Saturn: what it is, and why it is so named.*

As many of the wise men as have written of this masterpiece of philosophy <note>original: "Philoſophy"; referring here to Alchemy, the "Hermetic Philosophy"</note> have all spoken of the Rule <note>original: "Regimen"</note> of Saturn. Many, by misunderstanding this, have been led into various errors and have deceived themselves with their own opinions—some proceeding with a great deal of confidence, though with very little success. 

But know that our "Saturn" is more noble than any gold. It is the "slime" <note>original: "Limus"; referring to the primordial earth or the "First Matter"</note> in which the soul of our gold is joined with its Mercury, so that they may afterwards produce Adam and his wife, Eve. Therefore, that which is the highest shall so humble itself as to become the lowest; then, expect that he will redeem all his brothers by his blood. The sepulcher in which our King is buried is named Saturn in our work, and it is the key to the work of transmutation. Oh, happy is he who may behold this slow planet! Pray to God, my brother, that He would grant...

<summary>This chapter explains the "Rule of Saturn" in alchemy, identifying it not as the common metal lead, but as the essential "sepulcher" or dark stage of the process where the soul of gold is unified with mercury.</summary>
<keywords>Saturn, Regimen, Alchemy, Transmutation, Mercury, Limus, Gold, Symbolic Marriage</keywords>

### CHAPTER 23.

## *Of the various Regimens of this Work.*

Be certainly confident, studious Son of Art <note>original: "Son of Art"; a traditional term for an apprentice or practitioner of alchemy</note>, whoever you are, that nothing is hidden in this work except for the *Regimen* <note>original: "Regimen"; the specific method of governing the heat and timing during the alchemical process</note>. Regarding this, the saying of the Philosopher may be verified: "Whoever is master of that science, the princes and grandees of the earth shall honor him." 

I assure you, upon the word of an honest man, that if this one secret were but openly discovered, even fools would deride the Art; for once that is known, nothing remains but "the work of women and the play of children," <note>A common alchemical aphorism suggesting that once the initial difficult stages are passed, the rest requires only patient, gentle heating (decoction).</note> and that is *Decoction* <term>the process of heating or boiling a substance in a fluid to extract its essence or mature it</term>. So, it was not without cause that the wise men hid this secret with all their might. And rest assured that we have done the same, whatever we have seemed to speak concerning the de—

<summary>The author introduces Chapter 23, asserting that the true secret of alchemy lies in the "Regimen" (the management of heat), which is so simple that it would be mocked if revealed openly.</summary>
<keywords>Regimen, Decoction, Son of Art, secrecy, alchemical process, divine blessing</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's discussion on the practical stages of the alchemical process, specifically focusing on the transition from general theory to the specific "regimens" or stages of heat and transformation.</meta>

degree of heat; yet because I promised candor in this treatise, I must at least do something so that I do not disappoint the ingenious seeker of their hope and efforts. Know then, that our *regimen* <note>original: "Regimen"; here meaning a systematic method or prescribed course of treatment in the alchemical process</note>, from the beginning to the end, is only linear—and that is to decoct <note>to boil down or concentrate</note> and to digest. Yet this one *regimen* in itself encompasses many others, which the envious <note>The "envious" refers to secretive alchemists who intentionally hid their methods from others.</note> have concealed by giving them various names and describing them as many different operations.
<margin>The process is only one operation: that is, to digest or decoct.</margin>
We, to fulfill the candor we promised, will make a far more clear manifestation. So, reader, whoever you are (if you are ingenious), you shall find cause to acknowledge our honesty in this to be more than ordinary.

### CHAPTER 24.
## Of the first Regimen of the Work, which is of Mercury.

A young practitioner finds no small difficulty in mastering the primary signs of blackness. On this point, that good Marquis of *Treviso*, the noble *Bernard*, was silent; in his parables he says:

> "The King, when he came to the Fountain, leaving all strangers behind him, enters the bath alone, clothed in golden robes. These he takes off and gives to Saturn <note>original: ♄</note>, his first chamberlain, from whom he receives a suit of black velvet."

But he does not show how long the interval of time is before the King plucks off his golden garment. Therefore, he passes over in silence the first and most intricate *Regimen* <note>A "regimen" refers to a specific stage of the alchemical process governed by a particular planet or set of conditions.</note>, which lasts perhaps forty or fifty days before it is fully complete. 

<margin>
40 or 50 days, or 7 weeks until the blackness appears.
</margin>

During this time, the poor practitioner is left to uncertain experiments. From the first appearance of blackness until the very end of the Work, the sights that appear are sufficient to refresh the artist; but to wander without a guide or direction for the space of fifty days is, I confess, tedious. 

<margin>
He supposes you to have worked the common mercury <note>original: ☿ vulgi</note>, and out of it to get the Philosophical Gold <note>original: ☉ Philo[s]</note>. If proceeding with the calx <note>A powder produced by heating a metal</note> of Gold <note>original: ☉</note>, it kindles by the hissing of it—for this is very different.
</margin>

His companion being dead at first, and so remains for a great space of time; and this secret before me no man ever yet discovered:
<margin>Our Sun <note>symbol: ☉</note> refers to our matter, or it may mean common gold.</margin>
Therefore, when your matters are joined—which are our Sun <note>symbol: ☉; representing gold or the fixed principle</note> and our Mercury <note>symbol: ☿; representing the volatile principle</note>—do not think, as some *alchemists* vainly imagine, that the setting of the *Sun* <note>The "setting of the sun" refers to the blackening or "death" of the gold in the solvent.</note> will follow suddenly. No, truly; we waited a long and tedious while before a reconciliation was made between the water and the fire. This the envious <note>The "envious" was a common term for alchemical authors who hid their secrets from the uninitiated.</note> have mystically comprehended in a short speech when, in the first beginning of their work, they called their matter *Rebis*—that is, a thing made of two substances—according to the poet:

> *The Rebis is two things joined, yet it is nonetheless one;* <note>original Latin: Res Rebis est bina conjuncta, sed est tamen una</note>
> *It is dissolved, so that they become either the Sun or the seeds of the Moon.* <note>original Latin: Solvitur, ut prima sint aut Sol aut Spermata Lunæ. "Sun and Moon" here refer to the philosophical Gold and Silver.</note>

> *Rebis are two things joined, yet is but one,*
> *Dissolved, so that Gold or Silver be seed alone.*

<margin>This may also mean common gold or our matter.</margin>

# CHAPTER 25.

## Concerning the second *Rule* <note>original: "Regimen"; refers to a specific stage or method of treatment in alchemy</note> of the *Work*, which belongs to *Saturn*.

<margin>
Observe the word "Lion"; this explains page 6, line 8.

Having completed the *Rule* of Mercury <note>original: "☿"</note>, which is to strip the King of his golden robes, to assault the *Lion* <note>The "Lion" often represents fixed matter or gold that must be dissolved</note> with various conflicts, to weary him, and at length to kill him; the next *Rule* that appears is that of Saturn <note>original: "♄"; associated with lead and the color black</note>. For it is the will of God that the Work, once it has begun, should be carried on even until the end. The law of these operations is that the ending of one is the entrance into another; the conclusion of one is the beginning of another. No sooner does the *Rule* of Mercury pass away, but his <gloss>their</gloss> successor Saturn arrives, who is the next higher in succession. As the *Lion* dies, the *Crow* <note>The "Crow" or "Raven" symbolizes the *Nigredo* or black stage of alchemy</note> is born. This *Rule* is consistent in respect to its color, for there is only one single color, and that is the blackest black.

The matter will appear black, but there will be neither fumes, nor winds, nor any symbol of life. Only the compound will at some seasons appear dry, while at other times it will boil like melted pitch. Oh, sad sight—the very image of eternal death! 

But at the same time, this is a most pleasant messenger to the artist, for this blackness is not ordinary, but so intense that it actually shines because of its darkness. When you see your matter swelling beneath like dough, rejoice; for know that within this, there is shut a quickening <note>original: "quickening" meaning life-giving</note> spirit. In its appointed time, <gloss>20 days</gloss> this spirit will restore life from the Almighty to these carcasses <note>original: "Carkases," referring to the chemically "dead" or inert matter in the flask</note>. 

<margin>Nothing must be made to rise in this stage of the process.</margin>

## CHAPTER 28.
### Of the Regimen of Venus. <note>In alchemy, the "Regimen of Venus" refers to a specific stage of heating associated with the color green or a specific degree of fire.</note>

Above all things, this is most wonderful: that our Stone, being now wholly perfect and able to give a perfect Tincture <note>a substance capable of transforming base metals into gold or silver</note>, should of its own accord humble itself again and become volatile <note>evaporating or changing state easily</note> once more without any manual intervention. However, if you take the white stone out of the vessel, once it has cooled, it can never be brought into a new Operation even if placed in a new vessel. Neither we nor any of the ancient Philosophers are able to provide a logical reason for this; it is done only by the will of God. At this stage, be very cautious with your fire. It is the nature of the Stone, when it is perfect, that it must be fusible <note>capable of being melted</note>. Therefore, if you apply too much heat, the matter will be vitrified <note>turned into a glass-like substance</note>, and upon melting, it will stick to the sides of the vessel. If this happens, you cannot proceed any further with the Work. And this is the vitrifying of the matter so often warned against by the Philosophers, which often <insert>happens to them</insert> <unclear>who...</unclear>

<margin>Or the infant in her mother's womb, because they are united in each other.</margin>

## CHAPTER 31.

### The Fermentation of the Stone.

Remember now that you have obtained our red and incombustible sulfur <note>original: 🜍</note>, which cannot be advanced any further by itself through fire. Be very wary—something I should have told you in the previous chapter had I not forgotten it—concerning the *Rule* <note>original: Regimen</note> of the *Citrine Sun* <note>The "Citrine Sun" refers to a yellow stage of the alchemical process</note>. Before this supernatural gold <note>original: ☉</note> is born, which is adorned with a true *Tyrian* color <note>A deep purple-red dye highly prized in antiquity</note>, take care lest you vitrify <note>term: vitrifie; to turn into glass through excessive heat</note> your matter with too great a fire. If you do, it will thereafter be insoluble and, consequently, cannot be coagulated into these glorious *atoms*, red of the reddest. 

Be careful, then, that you do not destroy so great a treasure. Yet, do not think that your labor ends here; proceed further so that out of this sulfur, by repeated solution and coagulation, you may obtain our *Elixir*. 

Take, then, three parts of the finest gold and one part of this sulfur. You may take four parts of gold and a fifth part of our sulfur, but the previously mentioned proportion is better. Melt the gold in

<meta>The text continues on the next page with the process of melting the gold.</meta>

<summary>This page warns the alchemist to avoid overheating the "Red Sulfur" to prevent it from turning into glass, and provides the specific ratios of gold to sulfur needed to begin the fermentation of the Elixir.</summary>
<keywords>Fermentation, Red Sulfur, Alchemical Gold, Vitrification, Tyrian color, Elixir, Proportions</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions for the final stages of the alchemical process, specifically focusing on the creation of a "Mass" and the process of fermentation.</meta>

In a clean crucible, and when it is melted, put your <term>Sulphur</term> <note>original symbol: 🜍</note> into it, but do so very cautiously, lest you lose it to the smoke of the coals. Let them flow together, then pour them out into an ingot mold. You shall then have a mass which can be ground into powder, possessing a most glorious red color, though it will hardly be transparent. 

Then, take exactly one part of this finely powdered mass and two parts of your Sophical <term>Mercury</term> <note>original symbol: ☿; "Sophical" refers to "Philosophical" mercury, a specialized alchemical solvent</note>. Mix them well and place them in a glass vessel. Seal the glass and manage the heat as described before for two months. During this time, you shall see all the previously mentioned *regimens* <note>stages of transformation</note> pass in their proper order. This is true *fermentation*, which you may repeat again if you wish.

### CHAPTER 32.
## ***The Imbibition of the Stone.***

I know that many authors in this work take "fermentation" to mean the internal, invisible agent which they call "ferment." They believe that by the virtue of this ferment, the fleeting and subtle spirits are thickened of their own accord without any physical intervention. Our previously mentioned method of fermentation they call "cibation" <note>original: "Cibation"; the act of feeding the stone</note> with "bread and milk," as *Ripley* <note>George Ripley (c. 1415–1490), a famous English alchemist</note> does. However, since I am not in the habit of citing other authors, nor swearing by their words in a matter which I understand as well as they do, I have followed my own judgment in my writings.

<margin>
Observe this, for this is the natural work. It is the work performed upon our matter by constantly taking the "middle substance" and adding our mercury <note>original symbol: ☿</note> to it, then working it again and again. By "middle substance," I mean that which is above and perfectly united, for the gross, tasteless earth lies at the bottom and is useless.
</margin>

There is, then, another operation by which our Stone is increased in weight more than in virtue <note>meaning its physical mass increases more than its transformative power at this stage</note>. Take your sulphur <note>original symbol: 🜍</note>, whether white or red, and to three parts of the sulphur add a fourth part of the water. After a little blackness appears during six or seven days of decoction <note>the process of heating or boiling a substance in a liquid</note>, your newly added water shall be thickened until it is like your sulphur. Then add another fourth part—not in proportion to the whole compound, but...

<summary>The author describes the process of "imbibition," where liquid is gradually added to the alchemical sulphur to increase its weight, while distinguishing his terminology from that of other alchemists like George Ripley.</summary>
<keywords>Imbibition, Fermentation, Cibation, George Ripley, Sulphur, Mercury, Decoction, Middle Substance</keywords>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for the multiplication of the alchemical Stone through the process of imbibition (soaking) and coagulation.</meta>

The substance is now increased by a fourth part by the first imbibition <note>original: "Imbibition"; the process of a solid absorbing a liquid</note>. In reference to your first Mercury <note>original: "☿"</note> as you took it at the beginning—which being dried—add another fourth part, and let it be congealed with a suitable fire. 

Then, add two parts of the water in reference to the three parts of the Mercury which you took at first, before the first imbibition. In this proportion, soak and congeal three more times. At last, add five parts of water in the seventh imbibition, always remembering to calculate the water in reference to the Mercury as it was taken at the start. 

Seal your vessel, and in a fire similar to the previous one, make your compound pass through all the aforementioned regimens <note>the specific stages or "reigns" of the alchemical process, often associated with different colors</note>. This will be completed in one month, and then you have the true Stone of the third order; of which one part will fall upon a thousand <note>referring to the "projection" or transmutation ratio</note> and tint it perfectly.

<insert>
Item:
1. To 3/4 add 1/4, which makes 1 whole.
2. To 1 whole, add 1/4 of the same, which makes 1 and 1/4.
3. Keep the same 1/4 weight, making 1 and 2/4.
---
4. Then increase to 3/8, which makes 1 and 7/8.
5. The same 3/8, making 2 and 2/8.
6. The same 3/8, making 2 and 5/8.
7. Then add 5/4 (or 1 and 2/8), making 3 and 7/8.
</insert>

### CHAPTER 33.
## *The Multiplication of the Stone.*

To achieve this, no labor is required except that you take the Stone, it being already perfect, and join it with three parts—or at most four parts—of the <term>mercury</term> <note>original: ☿</note> used in our first work. Govern it with a proper fire in a vessel that is well-sealed. In this way, all the *regimens* <note>The sequential stages or "rules" of the chemical process, often associated with different colors.</note> shall pass with infinite pleasure, and you shall have the whole substance increased a thousandfold beyond what it was before its multiplication. 

If you repeat this work again, you shall run through all the *regimens* in three days, and your medicine shall be exalted to another thousandfold power of tincture <note>The ability of the Stone to transform or "dye" base metals into gold.</note>. If you repeat the work yet again, it will be perfected in a single natural day, and all the *regimens* and colors shall pass. Afterward, with another repetition, this will be accomplished in one hour. At last, you shall not be able to find the limit of the virtue of your Stone; it shall be so great that it will surpass your own ingenuity to calculate it, if...

<summary>This chapter explains the process of "Multiplication," where the completed Philosopher's Stone is combined with mercury and repeatedly refined to exponentially increase its quantity and transformative power.</summary>
<keywords>Multiplication, Philosopher's Stone, Mercury, Regimens, Tincture, Alchemical process, Potency</keywords>

## CHAPTER 34.

### *On the Method of Projection.*

Take your perfected Stone <note>The "Stone" refers to the Philosopher's Stone, the final goal of alchemy.</note>, either white or red, according to the quality of the Medicine you desire. Take four parts of either gold <note>original: ☉</note> or silver <note>original: ☽</note> and melt them in a clean crucible. Then, add your Stone—white or red, depending on which metal you have melted. Once they are well mixed together in fusion, pour them into an ingot mold. You will then have a mass that is brittle. 

Take one part of this brittle mass and ten parts of well-washed mercury <note>original: ☿</note>. Heat the mercury until it begins to crackle, then throw this mixture upon it; it will pierce the mercury in the twinkling of an eye. Increase your fire until it is melted, and the entire substance will become a Medicine of inferior virtue <note>The author describes a "multiplication" process where the Stone's power is diluted into a larger quantity of material to make it easier to use.</note>. 

Next, take one part of this new substance and cast it upon any purged and melted metal—specifically, as much as the substance can "tain" <note>original: teyn; meaning to tint, color, or transmute</note>. You shall then have the most pure gold <note>original: ☉</note> or silver <note>original: ☽</note>, purer than any that Nature can provide. However, it is better to perform the projection gradually, step by step, until the projection power ceases; in this way, the Medicine will extend much further. For when so little...

<summary>This chapter provides technical instructions for "Projection," the process of using the Philosopher's Stone to transmute mercury and other metals into pure gold or silver through a series of dilutions.</summary>
<keywords>Projection, Philosopher's Stone, Transmutation, Gold, Silver, Mercury, Alchemy, Crucible, Fusion</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the practical application of the alchemical "Medicine" and the importance of purity in the base metals used for transmutation.</meta>

Little is projected on so much, unless projection is made on Mercury <note>original: ☿</note>; there is a notable loss of the Medicine by reason of the dross <note>original: *Scorias*; the waste material or slag produced during smelting</note> which adheres to impure metals. By how much then the metals are better purged before projection, by so much more will the matter succeed.

## CHAPTER 35.
### On the Many Uses of this Medicine.

He who has once, by the blessing of God, perfectly attained this Art, I know not what else in the world he could wish for, except to be free from all the snares of wicked men so that he may serve God without distraction. It would be a vain thing to seek popular applause through outward pomp; such trifles are not valued by those who possess this Art—in fact, they despise them. 

Therefore, he whom God has blessed with this talent <note>original: "Talent"; here referring to both a natural ability and the biblical metaphor for entrusted wealth.</note> possesses a sense of contentment that far exceeds public admiration. First, even if he should live a thousand years and provide for a thousand men every day, he could never run out of means; he may increase his Stone at his pleasure, both in weight and in power. Consequently, if he so desired, a single man who is an Adept <note>original: "Adeptist"; a person who has attained the secret of the Great Work in alchemy.</note> could transmute all the base metals in the entire world into perfect gold and silver. Secondly, he may use this Art to create precious stones and gems,

<meta>The text continues on the next page with the word "such".</meta>

<summary>The author describes the spiritual and material benefits of the Philosopher's Stone, noting that an Adept gains infinite wealth and the ability to transmute all base metals into gold, yet should remain humble and focused on serving God.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Philosopher's Stone, Adept, Transmutation, Gold, Silver, Spiritual Contentment, Wealth</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's discourse on the powers of the Philosopher's Stone and provides a concluding prayer and a dated postscript regarding the work's composition.</meta>

# Secrets Revealed.

...such as cannot be paralleled in Nature, for goodness and greatness.

Thirdly and lastly, he has a Universal Medicine <note>original: "Medicine Universal"; refers to the Panacea or Elixir of Life</note>, both for prolonging life and curing all diseases, so that one true Adept <note>original: "Adeptist"; a person who has mastered the secrets of alchemy</note> can easily cure all the sick people in the world—I mean that his medicine is sufficient to do so.

Now to the King Eternal, Immortal, and sole Almighty, be everlasting praise for these his unspeakable gifts and invaluable treasures.

Whoever enjoys this talent <note>In the biblical sense of a gift or treasure entrusted to someone</note>, let him be sure to employ it for the glory of God and the good of his neighbors, lest he be found ungrateful to God his creditor, who has blessed him with so great a talent, and so on the last day be found guilty of misusing it and thus be condemned.

***

This work was begun in the year 1645 and ended by me, who have made and do profess these secrets, yet
